

=== Getting Started ===


--- Introduction to the Course ---

Hi everyone, and welcome to an interesting course on Splunk Beginner to Architect.
My name is Jill and I am going to be your instructor throughout this course.
So as part of this introductory video, we are going to discuss about some of the amazing features of
Splunk and why Splunk is so popular in the industry.
So with this, let's get started.
Now, before we discuss about the importance of Splunk in organizations, let's take a simple use case
of Demo Corp bank.
This will give us better understanding on why Splunk can be very useful.
So the use case states that you have a Demo Corp bank that operates hundreds of servers in its data
center.
Along with that, they are running various applications.
They have networking devices and firewalls.
Now, this is one of the very common things that you will see.
Maybe hundreds of servers might not be running in data center, but you might be working in an organization
where you have hundreds of servers that are running in some cloud provider can be AWS, Azure, GCP,
etc. so at an overview, similar setup is very common in most of the organizations.
Now one of the common challenges that comes when you have so many servers, so many applications is
related to monitoring.
So monitoring the application and the security logs for suspicious activity or for any other use case,
it requires you to manually logging into each server to review the logs.
For example, let's consider there is one server where things are not really working as expected.
Now what will give you the better understanding?
It is the logs.
So you'll have to first login to the server, look into the log to understand what problem is happening
and try to fix it accordingly.
Now this is a complete manual approach.
Maybe for one server or two server it is fine, but when you have hundreds of servers, this manual
approach is something that is not feasible.
So in such case, one of the potential solution that you will see being used in most of the organization
is a centralized analysis platform.
So an organization can push all of the logs and events from all of the servers to a centralized analysis
platform, and all of the team will log into the centralized analysis platform to look into the logs
and events.
And this makes your life simple.
Why?
Because now you don't really have to log in to each server, since each server is pushing everything
in terms of logs and events to a centralized platform over here.
So this centralized platform that we were just discussing is where Splunk is extensively used.
So at an overview, Splunk is one of the most popular log analysis and monitoring tools that is available
in the industry.
And one of the great benefits here is that it allows us to search, analyze, as well as visualize the
data from various devices that might be present in the organization.
So overall, it would look something similar to this.
So in the organization you will have so many servers.
You might be having AWS accounts, you might be running various applications and so on.
So all of the logs and events from everywhere you push to a centralized platform.
In this case it is Splunk.
And not only push the logs, you can generate great set of alerts, great set of alarms, dashboards
and so on.
So this is one of the true beauty of Splunk.
Now, just for reference, I have also added a screenshot which shows on how the log events really looks
like in Splunk.
So here you see these are all of the logs from the web servers that are appearing in Splunk.
Will also have a demo in just a moment.

in Splunk, you can generate great set of dashboards.
And these dashboards can prove to be very useful in the organizations that you will be working with.
Now, before we have a demo on Splunk, let's look into the three important benefits that the centralized
analysis platform provides.
One is that all of the metrics and logs from all of the devices can be analyzed centrally.
This is one of the core aspect of any centralized platform.
Second is you can generate dashboards, alarms, as well as alerts on suspicious activity or various
other use cases related to sales, etc. and third, you can correlate all the logs to find any attack
pattern.
So this again is very useful in many organizations.
So with this set let's go ahead and have a quick demo on Splunk.

However in larger organization you can have hundreds of servers.

For example here you see this is the IP address blocked for 20 minutes.
This is the IP address.
Why it is blocked.
Because they are trying to open some URIs like dot env which ideally they should not be trying to do
that.
It also shows you the country of Russia.
So behind the scenes what happens is that IP tables rule is created on the server.
And that IP table rule will block the specific IP address for 20 minutes in a similar way.

=== Splunk Architecture ===


--- Installation Methods for Splunk ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.
Now, in today's video we'll be discussing about
the installation aspects of Splunk.
Now there are two primary ways for installing Splunk.
The first one is where you go ahead and download
and install the Splunk Enterprise
using an installation package.
So installation package is like a single file
which we double click on
and the entire application gets installed for us.
So typically in Windows it ends with an extension of .msi.
And if we discuss about Mac
then you have a DMG based extension types.
So that is what the first approach is all about.
The second approach is where we go ahead
and download the Splunk Enterprise Docker image.
So the second approach is also quite popular nowadays
in the Enterprise.
However, the only challenge is
that you need to know the basics of Docker.
Now when we discuss about the first installation method,
which is via the installation package,
one of the great things here
is that the Splunk can be installed
in wide variety of operating systems.
So it can be installed in Windows,
it can be installed in Mac OS,
it can also be installed in Linux.
However, one of the caveats here
is that not all of the versions
of the operating systems are supported.
So let's quickly look into these.
So currently I have Splunk running.
However, this Splunk Enterprise is currently installed
in my Windows 10 operating system directly on my laptop.
So now what I can do is
I can go ahead and easily test various features of Splunk
within the local laptop
without really requiring the internet connectivity.
Now within my Windows 10, under the apps and features,
these are basically the apps that are currently installed
in my operating system.
So one among them, if I just search for Splunk,
you should see I have one app of Splunk Enterprise

=== Security Primer ===


--- Launching Infrastructure for Splunk ---

Instructor: Hey everyone and welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll go ahead and create
our infrastructure for Splunk installation.
So, to begin with a Splunk installation process,
we need one server, which is hosting
a Ubuntu operating system.
So, after we have this server up and running,
the second step would be to install Splunk.
Now, one more important thing that we need to notice,
that once the server is up and running,
what are the authentication types that we can use
to connect to the server?
Now, one of the most common one is
the password based authentication.
So, this is pretty straightforward to understand.
So, from your client, which would be your laptop,
you can go ahead and quickly connect
to your Linux server stating your username.
So it states, my username is admin and I want to log in.
Now, if a password based authentication
is enabled on the server,
then the server will respond back
asking you for your password.
So, once you supply your username
and you supply your password, your login will be successful,
and then you can begin the installation process.
Now, password based authentication is still supported
by a lot of cloud service providers,
but it is generally considered to be less secure.
Now, I still remember in one of the first organizations
that I used to work with,
I primarily had a role of a system administrator.
And, what most of the users they used to do
the login password for their computers.
They used to write it in a sticky note,
and they used to paste that sticky note
on the back of the keyboard.
And, most of the users used to do that.
So, this is one challenge, and the second challenge is
that most users would not really create a complex password
that is difficult to brute force into.
Nowadays, with modern GPUs, it is becoming much more faster
for the attacker to do a brute force attacks
on the system that supports password based authentication.
So for internet, this is something that should be avoided.
The second, idle authentication mechanism,
is a key based authentication.
So, in this type of authentication, what happens is that
there are two special keys that are generated.
First one is called as a public key
and second is called as a private key.
So, similar to what we see in the diagram,
you have a public key, you have a private key.
So, the basic concept is that the public key
is stored in the server and then a authentication mechanism
is associated with the key.
So, what will happen is, only when you try to log in
with the corresponding private key that is associated
with the public key store in the server,
will you be able to authenticate successfully.
Otherwise, not.
All right, so if you'll quickly look into the diagram.
The laptop client first tries to log in
with the username as admin.
The server, since it has a key based authentication,
so here you see, you have a public key which is present
on the server.
So, here it states that the server responds
that a password based authentication is not allowed.
So, you need to authenticate with a key.
So, the private key that is stored in the laptop.
Once this key is used as part of the authentication,
then the overall authentication can be successful.
Now, to quickly give you a demo,
I have two set of key pairs that I've created.
So, one is the public key, which is stored in the server,
and second is the private key.
So, if I'll quickly show you
the contents of the private key.
You see, the overall contents of the private key
is very big.
So, it is very difficult
that a attacker will be able to break it.
And, this is the reason why using the key based
authentication is much more recommended
than the password based ones.
So, for the server that we will be creating,
we will be using a key based authentication
for our use case.
So, one of the first step that will be implementing
is to create a public and a private key pair.
Once this set of key pairs are created,
we'll be creating a new server and we'll be linking it
with the public key that we have just created.
And then, we'll use the private key to log into the server.
So, to create a public and a private key
is pretty straightforward.
So, if I open up the command prompt over here.
So, this is Windows 10, you need to make sure
that the SSH related utilities are enabled.
So currently,
if I do SSH from the command prompt of Windows,
you see I am getting a usage information.
If you are using MacOS, things are pretty straightforward,
because SSH comes by default enabled within the terminal.
If you're using Microsoft Windows,
then you need to enable SSH explicitly.
So, if you'll quickly go
to add or remove programs over here.
Within here, you have a optional features.
And when you search for SSH, make sure that you enable
the open SSH related features over here.
The second option is you can use various terminals,
like, for example, you have mobile extern,
or even PUTTy that can also be used if you're using Windows.
So, for today's demo,
we'll be using a native feature of SSH.
So, the way in which we can create
a new public private key is to use SSH keygen.
Now, this command works perfectly well,
even for Mac, Linux, as well as Windows.
So, let me go ahead and press enter.
So, it is stating generating public and private key,
and it is also stating, where do we need to store this key?
So, I'll just press enter, this is the default location.
That is fine.
I'll press enter and great.
Here you see the SSH public, as well as the private key,
is stored in this specific path.
So, within my home directory here, you should see
a new .SSH file has been created,
and here you have a private key,
and here you have a public key.
That is great.
So, once we have the public and a private key,

Something very similar to what we had done in DigitalOcean.
Now, the third, and a very important part,
is related to the firewall rule.
Now, since our server is on the internet,
everyone across the internet will be able
to connect to our server.
So, this is something that we do not really want,
specifically when it comes to the SSH port.
So, what you need to do is with the help of firewall,
you can restrict the connection to your Splunk instance.
So, primarily, you will need two connections here,
port 22 and port 8000.
So, port 22 will be used for you to connect to the server
for your SSH,
and port 8000 is where the Splunk instance would be running.
So, what you can do is, for port 22 and for the port 8000,
you can manually specify your IP address,
so that no one in the internet,
except this specific IP address,
will be able to connect to the droplet here.
So, let's look into it.
Now, at this stage,
if I'll quickly open up the droplet over here.
Let's go to networking.
If I'll go a bit down, here you see it states
no firewalls are applied to the droplet.
So, basically, everyone from the internet,
including the attackers,
will be able to connect to the server.
This is something that you do not really want.
So, let's go ahead and attach a firewall here.
So, you can quickly create a new firewall rule.
Let's just call it as Splunk firewall.
And here for the SSH port,
currently all IPV4 and all IPV6 traffic is allowed.
Let's keep it this way just for a moment.
And, the second port that you might want to allow,
is the custom one, based on TCP,
and the port is 8000 where the Splunk instance listening.
Now, what you can do is,
instead of allowing the entire internet to connect here,
you can just do a what's my IP in Google,
and you'll get your IP address here.
You can copy the IP address.
And here, just paste the IP address
for both the port 8000 and 22.
All right, so now only this IP address
will be able to make connection to the server.
Outbound rule just allow the default ones,
whatever is applied here.
And, to apply to the droplet, let's just search it
by the name of our droplet, which is Splunk droplet.
We'll go ahead and create a firewall rule.
So, to test if things are working,
let's go ahead and log out.
So, you can type the log out command,
in case if you want to log out,
or you can press control+D in Windows.
Let's clear the screen.
Let's try to connect to the server.
And, if things are working well,
you'll be able to log in successfully.
Now, one important part to remember,
is that many of us will have dynamic IPs.
So, dynamic IPs is where once you restart your wifi router
the IP address keeps on changing.
So, this is bit of a pain.
So, every time your IP address change,
you'll have to manually edit the firewall rule,
so that you'll be able to connect to the instance
via your new IP.
So, in such cases, if that is something
that has been troublesome for you,
what you can do is you can allow port 8000
for the entire world.
However, just keep the port 22 to your IP.
So, this is much more secure,
because what happens is that in the internet,
most of the attackers will first target the port 22,
and they'll start the brute force attack.
So, this is one of the important port that we need to block.
In case if you do not block,
you can also explore lot of attacks,
which is also a very interesting project by itself.
And maybe, in our course related to the security monitoring,
we'll be discussing those in more detail.
So, internet is very interesting.
Specifically because of the attackers.

and understand the advantages that the Docker offers
let's look into a typical installation of software workflow.

experienced this with your work station.
Now, let's say that we have a Windows operating system
and you want to install certain application
or you want to install a certain game.
Now, the first step is to download the installer.

Now, during the middle of the installation
many of you might have already experienced
that you get a error message
during installation saying that certain files are missing

and once you install a specific dependency
you read on the installer and might be you get

as the previous one.

of troubleshooting it and rerunning the installer.

of workflow you typically see specifically when
you used to install the GTA game.

that there are certain DLLs which are missing
certain dependencies which are missing, and you had to go
through this software workflow at least a time
or two to make sure that you had all the dependencies
and all the requirements to install the game.
Now, this workflow that we were discussing earlier
this is the end user workflow.
Now, there is also workflow related to the development.
Now, let's say that there is a programmer who works very
hard at developing a software.
So he has developed this specific software.
Now, depending upon the way in which
he has created the software, it might or it might not,
work out of the box in the operating system.
So, let's say that he has created a software through
Visual Studio. So it might work directly in Windows,
it will not work in Linux, it will not work in Mac.
And this is a pain for the programmer,
because if he wants that software to work in Linux,
if he wants to make it work in Mac, it is really a pain.
He might use Java, but if let's say he does not
really want to use Java out of the box
it really becomes difficult for the developer as well.
So what he can do, he can create this software
He can put it in a Docker container.
Now he can deploy the Docker container in Windows,
He can deploy it in Linux, he can deploy it in Mac,
and he can be 100 percent sure that other
Docker container software that he has tested
in his local environment will work perfectly in Windows,
it will work perfectly in Linux, and it will work perfectly
in Mac and all the operating systems
which the Docker supports.
So, this really becomes much more easy
for the developer to push his software
to wide variety of operating system
and also all the dependencies which are required
by this specific software.
Because during the second slide that we are discussing
that a software installation typically fails due
to dependency issues, et cetera, he can put all
of these dependencies within the Docker container itself.
So, once he deploys the Docker container
to any operating system, it will work out of the box
and the end user does not really have to do anything other
than running this specific Docker container.
So, with this let's understand what
Docker is in definitive terms.
So Docker is basically an open platform.
Now, once we build a Docker container,
we can run it anywhere.
We can run it in Windows, we can run it in Linux,
we can run it in Mac, whether it is a laptop
it is in a data center, or it is in the Cloud.
Now, it follows a build once, run anywhere approach.
So that basically means that once the software
developer has built his own Docker container
he can run it anywhere.
He can run it in Windows, he can run it in Linux,
he can run it in Mac as well in a perfectly
working condition depending upon the task
that he has performed, while he has created a Docker
container.
So, this is the theoretical perspective.

--- Onboarding Data into Splunk ---

Hi everyone, and welcome back.
In today's video, we are going to discuss on how we can onboard the data to Splunk.
Now before we start to build amazing dashboards in Splunk, create lot of alerts, correlate data and
do lot of amazing things.
What is the first base prerequisite that is required?
And that is to add some data on Splunk.
So only after you have added the data to Splunk can you build alerts.
Can you build dashboards, can you correlate between data and so on.
So in today's video, we are going to discuss at an overview on what are the ways in which you can ingest
the data to Splunk.
And in the subsequent videos, we'll be exploring all of the ways in detail practically.
Now, one thing to note is that there are multiple ways in which you can add the data to Splunk.
One of the common ways that you will see in larger organizations is where you have Splunk Agent that
is installed on all of the servers.
It can be Linux servers, it can be windows servers, and so on.
And the Splunk agent pushes all of the logs to Splunk server.
The second way is you can also manually upload the log files to the Splunk instance.
This is possible, and third way is that Splunk can connect to third party platforms like AWS to fetch
the data.
Let's quickly look into all of the three ways through architectural perspective.
So the approach number one is where you have many servers like Linux servers on Linux server you install
Splunk agent, Splunk agent runs on the server, and whatever logs that are generated on the server.
These logs are pushed to the Splunk instance.
This is one of the very common ways.
Second way is that in case if you have some log files in your local computer, you can manually upload
the log files to Splunk instance for analysis and the approach number three.
Again, this is very common in organization is where Splunk can initiate the connections to third party
platforms like cloud provider.
It will pull the logs and it will perform the analysis on top of it.
Now what happens is let's say you have an AWS account, you will not be able to install the agent directly
on the AWS account, and this is the reason why you generally add the AWS account credentials inside
the Splunk and Splunk will regularly connect to your AWS account, fetch the latest data in events,
and then you can perform the necessary level of visualization and create alerts.
Now, within my Splunk Enterprise that we use in production environment.
Now again note this is a demo video.
Aim is to understand the concept.
So within here if I quickly go to the search and reporting app, and if you look into the data summary
for the production server, we have around 73,000 events that you are able to see.
So if I open this, you see there are so many events that are appearing over here.
Now, how are these events from the production server appearing as part of Splunk instance?
Answer is the approach number one, where you have Splunk Agent that is installed on the server and
this agent is pushing the logs to Splunk server.
Let me also quickly show you a demo.
Now let's do one thing.
There are a lot of logs that are being pushed.
We are interested in var log secure.
So generally var log secure.
What happens is when someone logs in to the SSH server, it can be a successful login.
Or if someone is trying to attempt to log in it can be a failed login.
All of the logs related to that will be appearing in this log file.
So what we are going to do let's go ahead and make it real time.
All right.
And from my local terminal let me connect to the production server.
Once again we'll do a live login over here.
So we are connected.
Now you see immediately in the Splunk server we got the logs.
So if you see it has accepted the public key and the session has been opened by root.
So now if I do a Ctrl D I log out.
Then you see again we got the error disconnected from user root session closed for user root.
So when agent is installed on the servers, some users might assume that it will take 5 to 10 minutes
for the logs of the server to be pushed to Splunk.
Answer is no.
It is very instantaneous.
We just had a demo on how fast it is.
So this again is one of the common approaches.
Second approach that we were discussing is the manual upload.
So within here what you can do is you can go ahead and add the data manually.
Let me show you an overview.

--- Basics of Search ---

Hi everyone and welcome back.
Now that we have uploaded the data to Splunk and also the field level extractions are happening automatically,
now we can proceed with the next and the interesting topic of searching the data in Splunk.
So we'll discuss on how we can search for a specific data in Splunk.
And at a later stage, we'll see on how we can use that to perform necessary level of visualization.
Now, in order to search for your data in Splunk, you make use of Splunk Processing Language, also
referred to as SPL.
At an overview, SPL allows us to search, analyze, as well as visualize the data within the Splunk
platform.
Now the SPL is very easy.
It is not some complicated thing that you have to learn.
If you are comfortable with, say, Linux commands or maybe basic of SQL, then understanding the SPL
will become very easy for you.
All right.
So at an overview, just remember that you will be able to search based on the SPL queries that you
create and based on that the necessary level of data will be provided to you.
This is the basics.
Now let's discuss about one of the simplest way to find a specific data.
In all of the events, you just add the data within the search over here, and all of the necessary
events where this value is present will be made available.
For example, here you are searching for 200.
So in all of the events in Splunk, wherever the 200 is present, all of the events will be made available
to you.
This is the simplistic form.
Now, one of the very important part whenever you search for data is the time range.
So for example, in larger organization where logs are present in Splunk, these logs can be from last
three years or last five years.
So when you are searching for a specific data, are you looking from past five years?
Most probably the answer would be no.
You might be looking for maybe past one day or past seven days.
For example, let's say there is some error that is coming and you want to search for that error within
Splunk.
So will you search for that error from past five years?
Answer is no.
You might be searching from say, past 30 minutes or past one year, or maybe today and so on.
So all of this is an important aspect to optimize your query.
So restricting or filtering your search criteria using time range is one of the most optimized way for
your searches.
So again note that let's say you decide that I want all time.
I do not really want one day.
Two day all time will definitely work.
But if Splunk is going to retrieve the log events from five years or ten years, it is going to take
time.
It is going to take a lot of CPU and Ram.
So unless and until you do not really require it, it is better to set the appropriate time range based
on the events that you are searching for.
All right.
This is one of the very important factors.
Before we proceed further, let's go ahead and try it out practically post which will be discussing
about few other areas.
Now going back to our Splunk instance, let's go to the search and reporting app over here.
And we already know that we had uploaded multiple set of data like the Linux Secure log or the access
logs and so on.
So the easiest way to search is you just add the value within the search over here.
So let's say I add 200 over here.
And again within the time range you have 24 hours.
You can select it to 30 days all time, etc. it depends upon your choice.
Let's select all time over here.
Since our data set is low, this is fine.
So when I click on search you see it is returning around 12,000 events over here.
So in all of the event that it returns you see the number 200 is present.
So this is one approach that we were discussing.
One more interesting thing is that it also shows you the timeline, for example 113 events on July 20th
or if you progress further 86 events on July 21st, and so on.
So this also helps in wide range of scenarios.
One more thing that it shows you is that these events are coming from multiple set of source types.
So 99% of events are coming from access logs, 0.9% of events are coming from Linux secure.
So this information is also very useful.
Now the next important area is related to filtering to the right source.
So here note that just by entering the value for the field that you are searching for in the search
bar causes the Splunk to scan all of the data, and this in turn can slow down your query and hence
it is necessary to optimize it.
Let us understand it.
So let's say that as a user we are searching for the number of 200.
Now we know that in Splunk we have two logs access logs and secure logs.
So if you just search for 200 over here, Splunk will go ahead and search in all of the log files,
and it will retrieve all of the events over here.
So this is the simplistic part, but are you really looking for this specific term in both of these
log files?
You have to decide.
Sometimes what happens is you are only looking for this term in say, secure log.
But if you just search for 200, it will even search for accesslog.
And sometimes in organization the Accesslog events are huge.
You have millions of events present over here, and this is the reason why, if Splunk is going to search
for millions of events in Accesslog unnecessarily, it will slow down your Splunk instance and it will
consume a lot of CPU and Ram.
So filtering to the right source is very important.
So let's say that you are only searching for 200 from the Linux secure logs.
So in this case what you can do is you can say search for number 200 but from secure dot log.
So in such case it will only search from secure dot log.
It will not search the potential millions of events that you might have in access logs, and this is
a much more optimized query.
Let's also look into it.
So within our Splunk instance, we had searched for the term 200.
And it is returning so many events over here.
But as we were discussing that we are only interested in this term but from the Linux secure logs.
So there are multiple ways to do that.
One is you can specify secure log over here.
So you can say source as secure log.
You can search it.
And now you see you are only having 108 events.
So not only this query will be faster, but your splunk will also not slow down.
And it is not necessary that you can only specify source as secure dot log.
In fact, let's go back.
You can also specify because all of the logs that are in secure dot log has a source type of Linux underscore
secure.
So you can also search by the source type of Linux underscore secure.
Let's search it.
And now again you see you have 108 events.
So this is a much more optimized query.
In production environments you should always run the optimized queries.
Now the next important area that we should be aware of is related to the Boolean expressions.
So the Splunk processing language which is SPL, it supports the Boolean operators like and or as well
as not.
And this will allow you to filter to the right set of events that are available.
For example, let's say that you want to search for all of the failed login attempts for the user root.
So in such case you can make use of the and operator.
So and operator will look for a event where both the term on the left and term on the right are present.
Second is search for failed logins for all users except root.
So in such case you can look for the term of failed and using the Not operator, it will look for all
the events that have failed, but the user of root is not present in them, then you can search for
failed logins for the user, admin or root.
So you can say failed admin or root.
So let's try it out.
Practically this will give us better clarity.
Now what you can do is for the events that are part of the Linux secure source type, you have various
field events as you can see over here.
So what you can do you can search for field from here.
But we know that what this will do, this will go ahead and search in both the access logs as well as
the secure logs.
So this is something that we do not want.
So we'll just add the source type of Linux secure so that it will only search for the secure logs.
So now we'll search for the term of field.
Let's press enter.
And now you see there are around 8000 events where you have the term field that is available.
So here your field password for invalid user app server failed password for invalid user test user.
So there are multiple user that someone is trying to login and the authentication has failed.
So all of these events are present.
Now let's consider the first use case where you want to search for all the failed login attempts, but
for a specific user of route.
So generally you will see that within here the user is being shown as app server.
So now what you can do.
You are searching for the term failed as well as the term route in a event.
So only the event where both failed is present and route is present.
Those events will be populated.
Let's click on search.
And now you see we have narrowed down the events from 8000 to 346.
So in all of the events you will see you have a failed route.
Failed route, failed route.
So this is one part, this is the importance of and and will make sure that both this term and this
term will be available in the log event that will be shown to you.
Second is search for failed logins for all users except route.
So here you can make use of the not.
So instead of and you can just specify not so all the events where you have a field but where root user
is not involved.
Those events will be provided to you.
So here you see failed password for invalid user app server, MongoDB desktop and so on.
So nowhere you will see root user in the events.
Third use case is search failed logins for user admin or root.
So now you are looking for failed events but from two users one user is admin.
So if I just search for admin you have around 212 events.
And if you search for root you have 346 events.
So now you're looking for all of the events where the user is either admin or root.
So you can add the query here you can search.
So now you see you have around 558 events that are available.
So either event will have the term field with the user of admin or field with the user of root.
Now, one more interesting part.
You cannot use a and over here in fact you can use it.
But this will not really show any events.
Why it will not show any events because any event will either have only one user.
It can either have root user or it can either have admin user or maybe any other user.
A single event will not have failed password from multiple set of users, because at a time only a single
user can log in as part of the session.
So this is the reason why and will not really work.
Over here you can specify something like or.
This is much more better.
Now before we conclude, one important point to note of Boolean operators is that the and operator is
always implied between terms.
For example, failed root is same as failed and root.
So unless you want to include it for clarity reason, you should not need to specify the term and in
the operator.
Let's take an example.
So we were discussing in the earlier query where we had added field and route.
So how many events were there?
346 events were there.
So it will search for event where both the term field and route are present.
Now the point states that you don't have to necessarily use an.
And even if you don't use an and it will use an implicit.
And so the and operator is always implied between terms.
So this is the first term.
This is the second term.
If you search like this again you have 346 events.
And if you search like this again you have 346 events.
So unless you want to include the and operator for clarity, reason, even if you skip it, it will
work perfectly well.

--- Solution - Finding Attack Vectors ---

Hi everyone, and welcome back.
Now in the earlier video, we had received some of the requirements by the auditor to find certain set
of data.
Today is going to be the solution video, where we'll try to see what is the probable solution that
you can use to fetch the necessary data.
Now the first requirement was find the total number of SSH failed login attempts.
Let's try to search for it.
First let's go ahead and search for the source of secure log where all of our Linux secure logs are
present.
Let's search for it.
Now.
One of the things that you will see is that you have a failed password for invalid user, failed password
for root user and so on.
So you have to first analyze the events.
Some events are like session open.
So this is where the user was able to successfully login to the server.
So these are the two primary events that you see session opened as well as the password.
Now you want to see find the total number of SSH failed login attempts.
How will you see the failed login attempts through this specific one.
So any event that has the field password.
You will consider that to be a failed login attempt.
So what you can do, you can search for failed password and out of around 9800 events, if I just press
enter here you see almost 8000 events are failed password.
So most of the events are failed.
Only certain events are successful.
So this is one of the SPL queries that you will use to find the data associated with requirement number
one.
Second is top attacking IP addresses.
Now for the Linux Secure log.
How will you find the attacking IP address?
One of the simplest way is to look into the failed attempts, because you see the majority of attempts
as part of the secure log are failed.
So all of these IP addresses, you can consider them to be the attacking IP addresses.
So you want to see the top attacking IP addresses.
So this will be simple.
What you'll have to do.
You have to find a field where the IP address is present in this case.
SK.
So now what you can do, you can just pipe it to say top SK.
And these are all of the top IP addresses that have failed password.

--- Overview of Splunk Reports ---

Hi everyone, and welcome back.
In today's video, we are going to discuss about Splunk reports.
Now at a high level overview.
Splunk report is a saved search query that runs on your data to generate a summarized results.
Now, as part of this course, we have been running some SPL query to generate some results like field
SSH, login attempts, etc. so this saved search query is nothing but the SPL query that gets run on
your index data in Splunk to generate summarized results similar to what we have been doing manually.
Now, one of the great benefits of Splunk report is that it can be scheduled to run at intervals, so
you can automatically schedule the specific save search query to run at 24 hours, weekly basis, monthly
basis, etc. and the results of it can be distributed via email, slack, webhooks, or even it can
be saved to dashboards.
And there are a lot of customizations that are available now before we look into the demo that will
give us better clarity, let's look into the workflow and then we'll have a demo.
Now one of the important part of Splunk report is the saved search query.
So this is the first part.
So whatever report you want the query needs to be created first.
So let's assume that there is a sales manager that requires the sales number on a daily basis.
So for that you will first require the SQL query that can find out the sales number.
Once you have the basic SQL query then you can create a report called as Daily Sales Number Report.
Now this report can be scheduled to run at regular intervals like 24 hours.
And also as we were discussing the results can be distributed via email, webhooks, etc..
So now once you have the report created, you can schedule it to be sent.
Whatever results that are output of this report, it can be sent over email at a 24 hour interval to
the sales manager.
And this entire process is automated.
So every day the report will get generated and the output of that report may be through PDF, CSV,
etc. will be sent to the sales manager.
This is the basic concept.
Let's have a demo to understand it in better terms.
Now for today's demo.
Currently I am in Splunk.
Let's go to the search and reporting app over here and let's go to reports.
Now within here you see there are multiple default reports that are created.
I also have this report which is failed logins by country.
You will not see it in your Splunk.
This is the custom report that I've created just for demo.
But you will see other reports that are available.
Now whenever you open up any report, let's open this.
Splunk errors last 24 hours.
You can open any report also.
So whenever I open a report you see in here I get the direct output.
I did not really had to run any SQL query etc. so this is one interesting part where directly you get
the necessary data without having to run the SQL query.
You can also customize time based on your requirement.
This is one part that is possible.
Now, we were discussing that a Splunk report is a saved search query behind a specific report.
For example, behind daily sales number report, there will be a SQL query that can find the sales number
in the first place.
Similarly behind this report also like Splunk errors in last 24 hours, there must be a SQL query.
So if I click on open in search, you will see that there is a SPL query that is available and the output
of this is given as part of the report.
Now again note that this is a simple query.
Generally in production environments you will have a very complicated queries.
So instead of just copy pasting those complicated queries to get the results, you can just create a
report, open the report and you get the direct output.
This is one of the great benefits.
Let's take one more example.
This is a custom example.
It is failed logins by country.
So from the name itself we can identify what it means.
If I'll open this over here automatically in the output you see on the left you have a country, and
on the right you have the failed logins.
So Australia failed logins is 132 and so on.
There are so many countries that are available.
Again for this.
We did not really had to write a SQL query from scratch, but we know that behind the scenes report
will have the SQL query.
If I open it in search, this is the actual SQL query that is available.
So this is one important part of a report.
Now the second important part of report is that it can be scheduled to run at intervals.
For example, this part that we were discussing that sales manager requires the daily sales number every
24 hours.
So here you have two approaches that you can use.
First is you can manually run this SQL query on a daily basis.
Whatever output of that query that you receive in terms of numbers, you can copy and you can manually
email to the sales manager at a 24 hour level.
But if you are lazy like me, you don't really want a lot of manual process.
You want everything to be automated so that even if you are in a vacation, you don't really have to
worry about sending the sales number automatically.
The Splunk will send the sales number and you can relax in your vacation.
So in a very similar way, if you look into this specific report, you will see that if I click on edit
you have a specific option of edit schedule.
So this report is scheduled to run every hour.
But you can run it every day.
You can run it every week, every month.
Or you can also have a cron schedule where you can customize the schedule accordingly.
All right.
So this is the schedule report functionality that we were discussing.
This is the great benefit here.
Then we were also discussing that the results of that report.
For example this report has certain results like how many countries and the associated failed attempts
are coming from them.
So this is the results that maybe a security manager would require.
So the results of this report can be sent to the appropriate manager through various channels like email,
webhook, etc..
Now, if I look into this specific report over here, you will see that within here it is scheduled
to be sent via email to a email address called as instructors at the rate.
And you also have the schedule priority like default higher, highest.
This is one interesting part.
So if you really require report daily basis on timely manner, you can schedule the priority to highest.
Now along with the email for example, if you see you can send email you can send to Splunk mobile.

--- Overview of Splunk Alerts ---

Hi everyone, and welcome back.
In today's video, we are going to discuss about one of the very important aspects of Splunk, which
is related to the alerting whenever you set up Splunk in production environments, if you do not have
the right set of alerts, then you will not be able to detect the security attacks in time.
So it is very important that you set the right set of alerts as part of Splunk.
This will help you tremendously.
So in today's video at an overview, we are going to discuss the basics of Splunk Alerts.
And later we'll also discuss about it from the practical perspective.
Now at a high level Overview Alert uses a saved search to look for events in real time or even based
on a schedule, generally for critical security related events.
The alerting works at a real time basis.
So this is first part.
Second part is alert triggers when search results meet specific conditions.
So let's take a simple example where you have a Splunk server.
And Splunk server is monitoring a specific Linux server that you might have.
So it is continuously monitoring it.
And now within here you have created a alert that will detect if the CPU utilization of your production
server is greater than 90%.
As soon as Splunk detects that CPU utilization is greater than 90%, it will immediately trigger an
alert, and this alert can be sent to the DevOps team who can monitor the server.
This is a very simple alert.
Generally, in larger environments you have very extensive alerts specifically related to security.
We'll be discussing it in just a moment.
Now for reference, I have also added a screenshot.
So generally when alert gets triggered, you will be able to see it in triggered alerts as part of Splunk.
However, you can also customize it where you can receive slack notifications, you can receive email,
and so on.
So with this base set, let's have a quick demo to understand this from real world perspective.
Now for today's video I have Splunk that is configured.
Now all of the traffic that is received as part of this.
Splunk is coming from KP labs website.
So whenever someone visits our website of KP labs in that traffic not only is logged at a server level,
but it is also forwarded to Splunk.
Now, the primary reason why I have a test setup of Splunk for KP labs is to monitor various kind of
security attacks that are happening on the server, so that I can also demonstrate it as part of seminars,
workshops, and definitely the videos as well.
Now, as part of this, if I go to search and reporting app, if you look into the data summary, you
see you have hundreds of thousands of events that are currently recorded and many of them are related
to attacks.
Now, let me quickly show you, I already have alerts that are configured over here.
Now one of the alert is related to IP block.
Now if I'll quickly open this alert, what generally happens is when someone tries to open some URL
like admin dot which they are not supposed to do, Then this alert gets triggered.
So if you will see in the actions over here you have add two triggered alerts.
And you also have slack.
So you have two actions that are happening over here.
And when you go ahead and go to triggered alerts in here, you see so many times the alerts are triggered.
That means some attacker are trying to open some web page as part of Collapsin, which either is not
existent, but the aim is that they are trying to open web page that they are not supposed to do.
This is one part.
In fact.
Let me also quickly show you.
So if I'll quickly go to the dashboard that we have.
Let me go a bit down.
So within here in request by country, you will see you have traffic that is coming from so many different
countries towards the KP labs in.
One more interesting thing that you will see is TCP IP addresses by count.
So some IP addresses are hitting.
you have 17,000 count.
So this is more of either a brute force attack that the user is trying to do or some form of denial
of service, etc. again, this is something that we are not going to discuss at this stage.
The most important part is this one page causing most errors.
Now you will see someone is trying to open URI for example in slash, admin slash env or in slash slash
env or slash dot slash config.
So generally dot git config can contain sensitive information.
So attacker is trying to see if there is this file that is available and accessible.
If they are able to see the file then it is a win win scenario.
If they are not able to see maybe you will get a 404 or maybe his IP address will get blocked.
So this is one thing.
From here we can see that there are so many attackers who are trying to go through the web server to
open various files and try their luck out.
So this is one part.
One more thing that we were discussing that for the alerts.
Not only they appear as part of the triggered alerts, but I also have a slack integration that is in
place.
So generally if I open up the slack you will be able to see.
So you see someone from Russia based on this IP address is trying to open.
So Klabin will not come over here.
Only the URI will come which is the slash dot env.
Or someone is trying to open this specific URI path or this specific URI path from various countries.
So this is one point that needs to be noted.
Let's have a quick demo to understand this entire setup as well.
So what I'm going to do through online curl, I am going to make a request to Cape Lab in and try to
see if there is a admin dot page that is available.
Many times what happens is that admin dot page is available and then the attackers will try to brute
force with username and passwords, or try to run various exploits, etc. but for that this page should
be available.
So when I make a curl request at this stage in slack notification, there is no admin that you can see
over here.
So let's click on run.
So as expected you have 404 not found.
However you see there is a new notification in slack where from United States this is the IP address.
And this IP address is trying to open the admin PHP page.
So why exactly all of this is coming so instantaneously?
It is because of the alerts.
Since you have alerts that are in place instantaneously over slack notification or through email, etc.,
you will be able to see the real time security attacks that might potentially be happening.
So this is the reason why having a right set of alerts is very important.
Now you see again, someone from United States is trying to open the admin form login.
So this is a very common occurrence.
Generally what we use to do is we used to immediately block the IP addresses in the firewall.
But I have kept it open so that we can see on what type of request the attackers are trying to make.
And this will help me subsequently as part of demos that I make.
So this is one part that needs to be noted.
Again, one thing is that in the triggered alerts you will see the same thing.
So the latest alert was related to form login.
So even in the triggered alert this is the latest alert over here.
So if you click on view result you will be able to see the exact one the admin form login.
So that's the overview about Splunk alerts.
Now again we are going to discuss about this practically in the upcoming videos.
But I hope at an overview you understood the importance of alerting.

which has a memory of 64GB and vCPU of 16.
Now do not, because this type of hardware
is definitely required for the Splunk add-on
for AWS considering you're pulling

that is already happening
and on top of that, using Splunk add-on,
you set a interval like 60 seconds
and Splunk add-on again will make a huge API call.

is also quite high.

Now at this stage we understand the directory structure of Splunk.
There are multiple files and folders that are available like bin SC you have opt, share, etc..

here.
Now one thing to note is that Splunk software uses the configuration files, which are primarily ending
with an extension of dot conf to determine nearly every aspect of this behavior.
And where are these conf files primarily stored?
They are primarily stored in SC.
So before we proceed further, let's go ahead and verify whether they are present in SC post, which
will be discussing about the configuration file precedence now in Splunk over here.

You have a sec.
Inside Etsy we saw that we have a system folder.
And inside system folder you have default and you have local.
So it can happen that there is a file called as server dot conf in the default, and a file with the

the higher precedence.

--- Introduction to Indexes ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video
we will be covering the basics about Splunk indexes.
Now in a very simple term
the index is a repository of Splunk data.
So whatever data that you put inside Splunk
it gets stored in a index.
Now Splunk transforms the incoming data into events
which in turn are stored in the indexes.
Now, once Splunk indexes your data
it creates number of files.
These files fall into two categories.
One is the raw data, which is in a compressed format
and second is the indexes that point to the raw data
which are also referred as the TSI DX files
which also contains some metadata related aspects.

everything is done in the passing pipeline.

--- Bucket Lifecycle ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video we will be discussing
about the Splunk bucket life cycle.
Now, we already know
from the previous video that Splunk basically stores all
of its data in directories and that directory
in technical term it is referred as the buckets.
Now a bucket, basically it moves
through several stages as it ages.
Now that stages are primarily hot, warm, cold, and frozen.
So let me give you one example.
So let's say that typically if you do searching in Splunk
you will or you might want to search
from last three days or from last seven days.
Typically, people will not search
for data like from last one year, right?
If you want to search from data
from last one year, that is something which very
few queries will have such requirement.
And this is the reason why Splunk will store
the data depending upon its age, so that the older data
it might be one year older
or whatever you specify it goes into disc
which is least expensive
because generally the data which needs to be searched
or which analysts would be searching quite
frequently it needs to be in a much more faster disc.
Otherwise there will be a lot of performance impact.
So what Splunk does is, any data which generally
is tended to be searched
it's it stores it in hot and warm bucket primarily
and then you can move it to cold bucket
which can be a different hard disk altogether.
However, the data which is in the cold bucket
it has little amount of chance of it being searched.
And the last is a frozen bucket.
So this is the overview about why
Splunk basically moves the data
into several stages depending
upon the age as well as various different factor.
Now coming about the bucket life cycle
you primarily have hot, warm, cold, frozen and thawed.
Now hot is basically any new data that is being
actively written to Splunk or any most recent data
it gets stored within the hot bucket.
Now data gets rolled from hot to warm
and a warm bucket are generally where no writing is allowed.
So one important point to remember is
that anything that you write
it only goes into the hot bucket.
Once the data goes into the warm bucket
it cannot be written to, alright
so hot is read plus write warm, cold
and others are only read only.
So you cannot write here.
Now once data goes from hot to warm
we know that data is not actively written to warm buckets.
Now after warm, it goes to cold.
Alright, so data gets rolled from warm bucket
to cold bucket.
Now the data which is there in the cold bucket, the
chances of it being searched by the analyst is little less.
So generally it gets rolled depending upon its
age or whatever configuration policy that you define.
Now from cold, it goes to frozen.
Now, frozen data are generally deleted by default
unless and until you tell Splunk to not delete
it or store it somewhere else.
Now from if, if you archive it
let's say you tell Splunk not to delete the frozen data
you archive it and if you want to restore the archive back
to Splunk, that process is called restore.
Now one important part to remember is
that typically in the organization which has compliance
they do not delete the data for compliance reasons.
So they might store the data
for for one year or even for five years.
And for this reason
typically frozen data will not be deleted.
So you'll have to explicitly tell Splunk not to
delete the data which goes
into frozen and archive it instead.
Now this is a nice little diagram
for the bucket life cycle so that we can understand.
So any events that comes into Splunk
it goes into the hot bucket.
Now, once the hot bucket is full
it goes into the warm bucket.
Now typically hot and warm bucket are in the same
hard disc right?
So you see they're typically in the same disc.
Now from warm bucket it goes into the cold bucket.
Now cold bucket you see it typically is a cheaper storage.
Now whatever disc you have in hot and warm bucket
it needs to be very fast
otherwise you'll have a lot of performance impact.
And this is the reason why the requirement of disc iops
or various other disc related performance matrix is very
high. Again, depending upon the data volume for hot
and warm bucket, however, you can have a little slower disc
for the data which is there in the cold bucket.
Now once data from cold bucket, it goes to frozen bucket.
Now a frozen bucket it
so any data that goes to frozen, it is deleted by default.
You can specify a frozen part
so that the data can be archived.
And then you have the thawed part.
So thawed part is you put the archived data
and you do the restoration process
so that it is again searchable in Splunk.
So this is the high level overview
about the bucket life cycle.
So this is the theoretical perspective.
Now, before we continue with more slides
again it will be more theoretical.
Let's do a practical and let's look
into how exactly this would look like.
So what we'll do is we'll go
to settings and we'll select indexes.
All right, so these are various indexes and
if you'll see each index here has its own max size.
It tells how many events are currently present
within the index, what is the earlier event
what is the latest event, the path
and also the frozen path.
Do remember that if you do not really specify frozen path
the data by default as we know it will get deleted.
So now let's go ahead and we'll create a new index.

--- Warm to Cold Bucket Migration ---

-: Hey everyone.
Hey everyone, and welcome back.
Now, in the earlier video we were basically discussing
about how data moves from hot bucket to warm bucket.
Now continuing the series,
in today's video, we will discuss
on how data moves from warm bucket to cold buckets.
Now, one important part to remember is
that ideally, historical data should be stored
in the cold bucket because
if you remember in the diagram that we were discussing,
the cold bucket path ideally should be in a cheaper storage.
So hot and warm bucket should be in a disc
which has a much more faster performance.
However, the cold bucket can be in a cheaper storage.
So where the discs are slow
but the storage gap SET is cheaper.
So this is how generally you will see lot
of organizations implementing the architecture.
Now, this is the reason why this point says
that ideally the historical data should go there
because if you search for the data which is present
within the cold bucket, your performance will be impacted.
Now basically buckets are ruled
from warm to cold when there are too many warm buckets.
Now, what do you mean
by when there are too many warm buckets?
Now, this is specified
within the indexes configuration, which you define.
So this is a sample index configuration where
you have your index name, you have your cold path
so this is the cold path.
This can be whatever path that you define.
It can be of current disc, it can be of remote disc.
And the last important configuration here
is max warm DB count is equal to 300, and that means
that there can be maximum of 300 warm buckets, after 300
warm bucket limit is reached
the bucket will be moved from warm to cold.
And in today's video, we will be looking
into this in practical aspect so
that we understand on how exactly it works.
All right, so I am in my Splunk CLI.
So we'll go to opt Splunk etc apps, search, local.
And within this directory you have a indexes.conf.
So let's open indexes.conf here,
and basically we have two indexes which are
present. One is KP labs, and second is bucket life cycle.
So bucket life cycle is the index that we are more
interested in.
Now, now within this index, you will see at the start
you have the cold path.
So this is the path where your cold database
buckets will be stored.
However, we do not have any configuration related
to maximum bucket that we were discussing.
So this specific configuration is not present.
So let's do one thing.

--- Thawing Process ---

-: Hey everyone, and welcome back.
Now, in today's video we will be discussing
about the last stage of the bucket life cycle,
which is for restoration.
Now generally the restoration is a manual process
and it is also referred as the thawing process.
Now, we already discussed that data
which is supposed to be deleted
can be moved to a frozen DB directory that we specify.
Now, if you want to restore the data
from the frozen DB back to Splunk,
there are certain steps that we need to perform.
Because if you remember the data in frozen DB,
it only contains a compressed format
which is journal.gz.
It does not really have any tsidx file,
or any of the meta data files.
So there are three steps which are required
as part of the thawing process.
One is moving that data from frozen DB to the TAR DB here.
So this is the TAR DB directory that we have
within our bucket.
We have to move our archive data here.
The second thing is you need to run
the Splunk rebuild command and you need to specify the part
of your restore archive that you want to index it again.
And third part is you have to do a Splunk restart.
So, these are the three steps

--- Overview of Universal Forwarders ---

-: Hi everyone and welcome back.
Now, in today's video
we will be discussing about
the Splunk universal forwarder.
Now generally in any organization,
one of the common things that
you will see is that users have laptop
and organization have set-up servers
where their application is running.
So when you want to send the logs
from the users laptops or from
the servers to the central Splunk,
then you need a specific agent.
And this is where
the universal forwarder
comes into the picture.
So at a high level overview,
the Splunk universal forwarder
is basically an agent
that collects the data
from a server or maybe from
a specific device and
sends it back to the Splunk.
So this can be better understood
with this diagram
where there are two servers
and each server has a
specific set of log files.
So when you want this log files
to be sent to Splunk you need
a agent that needs to be installed.
So this universal forwarder agent
you install in server one
you install it in server two
and this agent takes the log file
and sends it to the
central Splunk instance.
And from the central Splunk instance,
you can go ahead and
query the log files as well as
create the relevant set of alerts.
Now, do note that
in this specific diagram
we have considered servers
but it can also be various
other devices like laptops.
The Splunk universal forwarder
can also be installed
at a laptop level,
and all the log events
from the laptop can be
sent to the centralized Splunk.
So let's quickly look into
the basic architecture.
So you have a Splunk and
you have various set of devices
like you have a laptop,
you have servers, et cetera
where you install a universal forwarder.
And the universal forwarder
will take the logs from the device and
sends it to Splunk at
a specific receiving port.
So in this case,
a receiving port is triple nine seven.
So this port is where all
the universal forwarder agent
will send the logs.
And the logs that have been
received on this port will be
indexed at a Splunk level.
So this is the high level architecture
of how things work at a
universal forwarder level.
So for today's demo video,
I have two servers.
First is where our
Splunk instance is up.

And second server is where our

status Splunk is running.

go ahead and take various log files
from the wire log directory.
So there are various

So it'll take this file and
it'll send to the Splunk instance.
Now I already have a
Splunk instance up and running.
So if you'll go to the

--- Overview of Agent Management ---

Hi everyone, and welcome back.
In today's video we are going to discuss about agent management.
Now before we discuss about agent management, let's take a step back to understand the challenge that
organization generally faces with the universal forwarder based approach.
Now what happens is in larger enterprises you will have thousands of servers.
So you will have to monitor all of those servers in Splunk.
So what is one common thing that you will have to do.
You'll have to install Universal Forwarder in all of the servers.
This is fine through Ansible, chef, puppet, etc. you will be able to do that pretty quickly.
Now after installation of Universal Forwarder you will have to run certain commands.
So in our practical video of Universal Forwarder installation in Splunk, we had ran two primary commands.
One was the Splunk ad monitor var log and then the Splunk ad forward server, followed by the IP address
of the server where the log file needs to be sent to.
Now this is also fine, and maybe one time you can automate this as part of the ansible or chef.
Now the issue that happens is, let's say after one week, there is one more requirement to monitor
an additional log file.
So let's consider that there is a log file.
And you want to monitor that in all of the servers.
So now what you will have to do in all of the thousands of servers that you might have, you will have
to create an Ansible playbook or a chef recipe that will go ahead and get deployed and will run the
add monitor command based on the newer path.
Now, after one month, maybe you have one more log file that you want to monitor.
Again, you will have to run Ansible on all thousands of servers and so on.
So this is something that you do not really want because for each modification at a universal forwarder
level, you have to run a ansible or some form of automation in all thousands of servers.
And this is something that is not the best of the approaches.
Again, again, a lot of organizations still uses that approach, but there is a better approach that
is in-built and available in Splunk.
Call as agent management at a high level.
Overview.
Agent management allows us to centrally deploy changes to all the universal forwarders, eliminating
the need to manually execute commands on each individual forwarder.
We'll be discussing about this in just a moment, but the aim here is that there is a central feature
in Splunk through which you can deploy all of these changes.
No longer you have to run Ansible manually on all of those servers.
So this is the basics.
Now let's discuss about the basic architecture of agent management at a high level.
Overview.
The agent management feature in Splunk is used for managing the data collection agents.
So these are basically the universal forwarders that you might have installed in your servers.
Again, you have a wide variety of support that is also available over here.
And the primary purpose of agent management is to manage the large group of forwarders that you might
have.
So in Splunk you enable the agent management feature, and now in the agent management you can set various
set of configurations.
Now these configurations can be very detailed.
For example, you can say that if there is a server whose IP address is starting from 192 star, so
192, one six, eight, 10.5, etc. for those servers, monitor only var log secure.
Then if there are servers whose IP addresses are starting from 10.77 monitor var log secure as well
as var log audit, or if there are servers whose hostname is something similar to win star monitor windows
events as well as the Active Directory logs.
So all of these customizations you can do at the agent management level.
And this is of great help in enterprises.
Now discussing about agent and agent management.
Note that agents can be universal forwarders.
In most of the cases is universal forwarders are the agents that are actively managed.
It can also be heavy forwarders.
It can also be indexers, search heads or various others.
So this is one point to note.
So if you look into the screenshot, there are two servers that are managed as part of agent management.
One is the backend server zero one, backend server zero two.
So the status is also showing as okay.
And the agent type you can clearly see as universal forwarder.
Let me also quickly show you a demo.
This will give us better clarity.
So currently I am in my demo Splunk instance.
Now as part of this, if we go to the agent management over here, you see within here you are able
to see two servers where the universal forwarder agent is installed.
The agent type here is universal Forwarder.
The status is also okay.
Now this is very beneficial.
So generally in larger enterprises you will have thousands of servers.
So you would like to see the status of universal forwarder whether it is working it is not working etc.
so you will be able to see the status from here.
And in case out of 1000 servers, maybe there can be 2 or 3 servers where universal forward is not working
as expected.
You can quickly identify from here and then you can troubleshoot by logging into the server.
So this again is very beneficial.
Second thing that we were discussing is that you can centrally manage them.
For example we have one application here which is sample underscore app.
So within the application you can configure what log files you want to monitor.
So for example let's say in the sample app you have specified I want to monitor var log.
But after one week there is a requirement to monitor app log.
So all you have to do is you have to deploy a new application centrally from here to all of the universal
forwarders.
And that's about it.
Splunk Agent Management will take care of everything in a similar way.
If you have deployed an app to the universal forwarder and you do not want that app, now, you want
to remove it.
That is also something that is possible.
Let me in fact show you.
So at this stage, you see we have a sample app that is available.
The deployment status is successfully deployed in two agents because we only have two servers that are
connected as part of the agent management.
So let's say that I want to remove this sample app.
So this again is simple.
So I can click on uninstall.
Let's refresh.
So in the agents you see this time you only have one.
So it is removing this app in all of the universal forwarder based agents.
Let's refresh again.
And you see in the agents it is zero.
So the deployment status at this stage is not deployed.
So from the centralized console it becomes very simple to go ahead and manage things through the agent
management feature.
Now before we conclude one more thing that I wanted to show you.
What happens if one of the servers may be the universal forwarder agent stops working, or the server
itself stops working?
How does the status really looks like?
Let me also show you so that we get better clarity from here.

the Splunk as part of this.
All right.

also.

So from here you can quickly diagnose if there are issues with the universal forwarders.
And then once you see the status as offline, you can quickly troubleshoot by logging into the server
to see what is the issue.
And also your agents offline.
It is showing as one.
So this proves to be very useful over here.
So this is one more part related to the agent management.
Now before we conclude few pointers that we have to keep a note of, first important one is that starting
with Splunk version 10.0 onwards, Splunk has made some changes towards the product names.
So Deployment Server is what we use to refer earlier.
However, now it is called as agent Management and also forwarder management is now also called as agent
management.
So if you discuss about Splunk Splunk nine and then you compare it with Splunk ten, then there will
be some terminology differences as well as the option names might be different as well.
But if you hear someone say deployment server, it is very similar to what we were discussing in today's
video.
One more thing that I wanted to share is that in the earlier versions of Splunk here, the name was
Forwarder management.
It was not agent management.
So for example, if you discuss about Splunk eight you had option of forwarder management.
Now it is referred to as agent management.
And again there are slight changes in GUI as well as some features are also additionally added.
So naming convention is something that you have to be careful about.
So in case if you hear deployment servers in the documentation or in some older videos, you do not
really need to get confused.
So this is the basics of agent management.
I hope at an overview you have started to understand what it is all about.
In case if there are any confusions, then in the upcoming videos, we are going to discuss in more
detail about this specific feature along with practicals, and that will make things much more clear.
So that's the overview.
And with this we'll conclude today's video.

--- Important Concepts - Server Class and Deployment Apps ---

Hi everyone and welcome back.
So in today's video, we are going to discuss in more detail about the agent management in Splunk.
Specifically, we are going to discuss about the server class and deployment app.
If you understand the server class and deployment app, then the overall feature of agent management
will become much more simpler for you to understand internally.
Now, before we do that, if you remember in the earlier set of videos, we had set up the Splunk Universal
Forwarder and there were two important commands that we ran.
One was the Splunk ad monitor followed by the path, and second one was the Splunk ad server, followed
by the IP address of Splunk server, to which the logs needs to be sent to along with the port of 9997.
Now, although this is easy to understand, but one more thing that we need to know is that internally,
whenever you run this command, what Splunk will do behind the scenes, it will create a file called
as inputs dot conf with the exact path that you want to monitor.
It can be var very long.
It can be tmp app dot log.
Now since in this specific command we have add monitor tmp app dot log.
So even though you run the CLI command behind the scene, Splunk will go ahead and create a input.txt
conf file.
And within here you will have a monitor on this specific path over here in a similar way, in the second
command of Add forward server.
Forward server is a server to which the log needs to be forwarded to.
So here you define the log, but to which Splunk server the log needs to be forwarded to.
There must be some form of IP address of that Splunk server.
So this is where the second command comes into picture.
So when you run this command behind the scenes internally there will be one more file of outputs conf
that will be created.
And this outputs dot conf will have this specific IP address of Splunk server along with the port.
So this is one important point that we have to remember.

Overview.
The deployment application is a set of content.
It is primarily the configuration file maintained on the agent management server.
So whatever Splunk instance is present that is acting as an agent management, the deployment app is
maintained over there, and from that it is deployed to all of the universal forwarders depending upon
the requirement.
Now let us understand what this deployment app does.
So generally let's consider that you have a deployment app of Linux underscore logs.
So now in this deployment app you can go ahead and define the inputs dot conf which will define what
are the log files that you want to monitor.
For example here you have defined that I want to monitor var log.
So this is something that you can define as part of this deployment app.
And then you can also define the outputs dot conf that once this log files are monitored to which Splunk
server do you want the logs to be forwarded to.
So this is the outputs dot conf.
Now again a deployment app can contain other configurations also.
But we are keeping it simple to the core that it can contain inputs dot conf and it can contain outputs
dot conf.
I hope you understand the importance of both inputs and outputs.
The second important area is the server class at an overview.
You can use the server class to map a group of agents to one or more deployment apps.
So let's say that you have created a deployment app of Linux underscore logs.
And this has the inputs and the outputs dot conf.
So in order to install this deployment apps to the universal forwarder agent, what you'll have to do
you need a server class.
So this server class will be connected to deployment app.
It will also be connected to the servers.
So to whatever servers the server class is connected to.
Depending upon the filters, this deployment app will get installed over here.
And then whatever logs that you are monitoring those logs will be collected and it will be sent to the
server that is part of the outputs dot conf.
Now again, note that it is not necessary that a server class needs to connect to all of the forwarders.
You can go ahead and create a filter which states that only this specific deployment app should only
go to the server one and server two.
So a lot of flexibility is available over here.

--- Splunk Event Types ---

Instructor: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video we will be discussing about the basics
related to Splunk event types.
Now event types are basically categorization
which can help you make sense of your data.
So let's understand this with an example.

type is equal to access underscore combine status is equal
to 200, and action is equal to purchase.

and the status of 200, which which means it was a success.

save this as a event type call it
success_purchase.

you can just do a event type physical to success
underscore purchase and you'll get all the result
which has this specific events.
Now do remember this is not,
although it might look similar to saved search,
it is not because any event that gets returned,

--- Tags ---

--- Access Control ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.

about the access control in Splunk.
Now depending upon the use cases

the way in which you should be taking care

sensitive data where even having access

use a isolated, separate Splunk instance

a different VPN connection altogether.

of requirement you might have, you will have to
consider using a separate Splunk instance altogether.

not extremely sensitive data,
then maybe you can restrict access based
on index to the user.
We'll be discussing more

with the user is associated with.
