--- Overview of Distributed Splunk Architecture ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video we'll be discussing
about the distributor Splunk Architecture.
Now a Splunk Enterprise that we generally use
it consists of various other sub components as well.
Now, this includes, now the Splunk Enterprise that we use,
it consists of various sub components.
Now these sub components includes,
Indexer, you have Searchhead, you have deployment server
you have forwarders, you have license master, you have,
you even have monitoring console.
So simple example I can give you is of my mobile here.
So although this mobile is a complete package
but it consists of various other sub components.
So if you'll see, oh yeah, my motto, Motorola phone
it contains this big camera at the back.
So this is a external component
although what they do is they fit together
and it becomes a a single unit.
And similar goes with the Splunk.
Splunk also con contains various other components.
They can be distributed.
However, if you just install Splunk,
all of this forms a single unit which you can use
within a single Splunk Enterprise installation.
So let's understand this with a simple animation.
So let's assume that the entire blog that you see over here
it's a Splunk Enterprise.
Now, within this you have various sub components
like this can be Indexer, you have searches,
monitoring console, deployment,
server forwarders, et cetera, et cetera.
Now if you use a single Splunk Enterprise instance here
it might happen that one of the components might fail.
And many times what happens is
that if you install everything within a single server
this failure can cause the failure
of the entire Splunk instance itself.
So you need to be careful specifically when you
are using Splunk in your corporate environment
where the searchability is a important aspect.
You don't want Splunk to be going down
and hence having a single Enterprise instance
is something which is not preferred.
So what you do here is you do a distributed setup.
So in distributed setup, what you do is
individual Splunk Enterprise instance is used
for a single component.
So let's say this one can be used only for Indexes.
This can be used for Searchheads,
this can be used for for orders.
This is exclusive for
monitoring console, et cetera et cetera.
And for such cases, if one of the component fails
it will ensure that rest of the component will not
be affected totally because this is
in a isolated Splunk instance.
All right, so many times
distributed Splunk components really helps a lot.
However, do not consider distributed Splunk
or distributed setup as the best setup
because even if this component fails at this amount of time,
let's say, "currently this component has failed,
a component B has failed."
So during that time
what will happen is, I'll not be able to utilize the feature
of the component B, whatever it may be.
It may be Searchhead,
it may be Indexer, it may be forwarder.
But during the time this is in the fail state
although it might not bring all the other components down
but it will, we will not be able to use the feature
of the fail component.
And hence distribute setup
by itself is not a very ideal approach
and hence we make use of something like clustered setup.
So what happens in clustered setup is you have
a cluster of individual components.
So say you have a cluster of Searchhead,
so you have a cluster of Searchhead,
you have a cluster of Indexer,
you have a cluster of forward as well.
So in this kind of a setup, even if one,
if one instance within the cluster goes down
your component is still not down.
Alright? So now let's say the second instance
of your component has gone down
You still have your third instance of your component up
and your functionality will still be preserved.
And this is the importance of a clustered setup.
Remember that if you're going to install or if
you're going to run Splunk in your production environment
having a clustered setup is very,
very important to ensure that all the functionality
of Splunk is up and available all the time.
So although we were discussing in terms of various symbols
over here, when you talk about clustered setup
you, you need to have a systematic diagram
about how exactly it works.
So if you see, you have a three Indexer nodes in cluster,
Indexer are basically it's used for indexing the data.
Then you have the Searchhead cluster
which is basically used for Searchhead component.
And if you see how Indexer cluster interacts
with the Searchhead cluster.
So this is what the cluster set up looks like.
When it terms of the technical diagram,
we'll be doing the entire cluster.
We'll be looking into how we can create Indexer cluster.
We'll be looking into how we can create Searchhead cluster
in the upcoming section.
But I just would like to show you on how exactly
this would look like in terms of technical diagram.
Now, before we go ahead and do all of these clustered set up
it is important for us to understand what each
and every components within Splunk is all about.
So we already discussed
that Splunk Enterprise contains various sub-components
which can be Indexer, Searchhead, deployment,
server license, master, forward, monitoring console.
So before we do a clustering
before we do high availability, it is important
for us to understand what this component is
because if we do not understand
we'll not understand the true motive
of doing a clustering setup.
So throughout this section
we will be understanding each of these components in detail.
Post witch we'll look into the clustering
or maybe a high availability setup as well.
Now, one very important part to remember is
that Splunk does not support clustering feature
for each and every component, which is present over here.
Now two of the major components within Splunk
which supports clustering is Indexer and Searchhead.
And our next two sections are dedicated
for achieving clustering for both Indexer and Searchheads.
So this is very important part,
for other components like license, master, heavy, forwarder,
Splunk does not support clustering
It supports active passive based failover.
So this is one important part to remember.

Do not distribute.

of the licensing volume.

--- Indexer ---

Speaker: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing
about one of the very important components of Splunk
which is Indexer.
Now, Indexer is a component in Splunk enterprise
whose responsibility is to index data,
transform the data into searchable events,
and then place the results into the appropriate index.
Now, generally what happens is that
since this is such an important component,
typically to ensure high availability,
Splunk provides an out of the box clustering capabilities,
which most of the organizations who are running Splunk
typically opt for.
Now, in order to understand the Indexer component
let's take this diagram where you can have multiple inputs.
So you can have a monitor input
you can have a FIF four UDP input
DCP input, scripted inputs, et cetera, et cetera.
Now, from these inputs, the data comes from parsing queue.
Now this is the queue from queue.
It comes to the parsing pipeline.
Now, in the parsing pipeline, lot of things like source,
even tagging the character normalization,
the reg transformation, everything happens here.
Now, once everything related to parsing happens,
the data then goes to the index queue.
From index queue, it goes to the indexing pipeline
where the index building happens.
And once this is completed, then the data,
which is the raw data, as well as the index file
gets stored within the raw disk.
So there are two stage, which generally offers over year.
One is the parsing stage, and second is the indexing stage.
So parsing queue and parsing pipeline,
they formulate the parsing stage.
The indexing queue, and indexing pipeline,
they formulate the indexing stage.
Now, let's understand the importance of both of these.
The first one is the parsing stage where let's look
into some of the actions which happens
during the parsing stage.
One of the first actions that happens is the extraction
of the default fields of each event,
which includes host source and source types.
So let's understand this before we proceed further.
So typically, this is my Splunk enterprise instance,
and whenever you open any event, you would see
that there'll always be three fields.
One is host, second is source, and third is source type.
So this is also referred as the default fields.
And then you have the event fields here.
So you have the default fields and the event fields.
This parsing of whole source and source type happens
at the parsing level.
Now, along with that,
basically if you go to settings, and if you go
into the data inputs,
let me quickly open up the diagram.
You see there are multiple inputs
from which the data can come into the parsing queue
and this inputs are something that we see.
Yeah, you have TCP, UDP, you have scripts, you have a HGC
and you also have a monitor where you can monitor
the files and directories.
So once this extraction happens,
you have various other capabilities which are part
of the parsing queue.
One is the configuring the character set, encoding,
identifying the line termination
using various line break rules, which you can define
or with Splunk and do it by themselves.
Next, one of the important things that lot
of organization uses is to mask the sensitive details
in the data, like credit card numbers which might come.
So these are some of the functionalities
of the parsing stage.
Second is during the indexing pipeline stage,
Splunk performs various aspects like breaking events
into segments, which can later be searched upon,
building index data structures, which is important
for quick search, as well as writing raw data
into index files to the discs.
So these are some of the aspects of the indexing stage.
So these are the two important states that happens
and all of these are part of the indexer component
which takes care of all these aspects.
So this is the high level overview
about the indexer component.
In the upcoming sections, we will be discussing
in detail related to how we can ensure high availability
with the help of clustering of the indexes.
But for this video that's about it.
I hope this video has been informative for you,
and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Masking Sensitive Data at Index Time ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.

into the masking of sensitive data before it gets indexed.
Now typically it might happen that whatever log file
that you are ingesting, it would contain,
or it might contain, certain sensitive information
like credit card information or social security numbers

--- Search Head ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be discussing
about the search head component in Splunk.
Now, basically, search head is a component
in Splunk Enterprise whose responsibility it is
to manage the search related functions.
It also is responsible for sending the search request
to the responsible indexers, retrieving the results,
and merging the results before it sends back to the user.
So this can be explained with the below diagram
where you have multiple indexer nodes over here,
and then you have a search head.
So this search head component will send the request
to the indexer node.
Indexer node, I hope you remember,
it is responsible for a lot of functions.
The primary ones are related to parsing and indexing.
So a search head will not contain any data.
It will send the request to the indexers,
indexers will fetch the request,
depending upon the request
which the search head component gives,
it sends the request back to the search head,
search head will merge all the results,
and send it back to the user.
So that is the primary responsibility of the search head.
Now, typically when you are doing a clustered setup
or even a distributed setup, what you typically have,
you'll have a search head cluster
and you'll have the indexer cluster.
Now, indexer, by the indexer, you'll see all the forwarders.
It may be universal forwarders.
It will send the data to the indexer nodes over here
and the data will be stored in this specific cluster.
All right?
So no index data will be stored in the search head cluster.
All the index data will be stored in the indexer cluster.
Now, in order to search this cluster,
we make use of search head.
Now, if you'll see this diagram,
one search head member over here,
it connects to multiple indexer nodes
because it might happen that typically,
when you have a clustered setup,
20% of the data might be in indexer 1,
say 20% might be in indexer 2,
20% might be in indexer 3.
So typically when you search,
it needs to be searched across the indexer nodes.
So search head typically will send the data
to all of the indexer nodes, it retrieves the result,
and again, the user will be able to find those result sets
within the search head member.
So when it comes to the search head,
search heads are used for a number of functions.
Some of the primary one includes
definitely search related functions,
building dashboards and reports as required.
It is also used for building data models
or data model acceleration,
and definitely the alerting related functionalities
also built within the search head.
So typically if you have this type of architecture,
the users will not search the indexer nodes here.
They will log in to the Splunk instance,
which is part of the search head cluster,
they'll put their SPL query here,
search head will interact with N number
of indexer peer nodes which are present in the cluster,
retrieve the result, and send it back to the user.
So that is the primary component of search head function.
So, in the upcoming sections,
we will be discussing upon how we can formulate
the search head cluster after the indexer cluster is built.
So we have two sections which are dedicated
for the indexer cluster as well as the search head cluster.
So that's about the basics
about the search head as the component.
I hope this video has been informative for you,
and I look forward to seeing you in the next video.

--- Overview of Indexer Clustering ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing at a high level
overview about the indexer clustering.
Now, in the previous section we were discussing
about indexer as a component.
Now, one important part to remember is that till now,
we have been doing all the activities
within a single Splunk instance.
And let's say whatever data you have
in your Splunk instance and your Splunk instance goes down
then typically what would happen is your data would
either be lost or it would be inaccessible
till you bring back your Splunk.
So this is the reason why for corporate environment,
it is very important to ensure
that a high availability is present
and this can be achieved with the help of indexer cluster.
So within your Splunk enterprise instance,
if you go to settings,
and if you click on indexer clustering,
you see you have a option for enabling indexer clustering.
So by default, you do not have this enabled.
So if you click on enable indexer clustering,
there are three options that you'll be presented with.
One is the master node, second is the peer node,
and third is the search head nodes.
So let's understand what these mean.
Now, master node basically coordinates the activities
of peer node.
And peer node are basically the nodes
which contains the actual data
and it performs the replication related activities
or any activities which master node
tells the peer node to do.
Now we can understand this
with a simple indexer cluster architecture,
where you have one master node here
and you have two peer nodes which are present.
Now, depending upon the data size that you might have
for your organization,
the amount of peer nodes that you would need
typically would vary.
Now, currently you have two peer nodes here
but there are organizations who have 10
or even 20 indexer peer nodes.
So when you have a larger indexer peer node,
let's say you have 10 indexer peer nodes,
there needs to be some leader
who can inform about the configurations,
about the settings to all of the peer nodes.
And that leader who dictates the configuration,
the search factor related, the replication factor related,
and various other configuration settings.
So that is called as the master indexer.
So master indexer is responsible to inform each
and every peer node about the configuration set
that they should be adhering to.
And this is the reason why, typically you would be asked
whether this node that you are configuring,
whether you want it to be a master node
or you want it to be a peer node.
Now, search head node is a different aspect altogether,
which is basically used for searching.
Now when it comes to pure indexer clustering,
you would need a master node and you would need a peer node.
These are the only two aspects that you will be working it,
whenever you are working on indexer clustering.
Now, one more important part that I would like to share
before we conclude this video is that
what master indexer or the master node
does not take part in the replication by itself.
So let's say you add a data to indexer node zero one,
it'll get replicated to indexer node zero two
or it can even get replicated
to other indexer node which are present within the cluster
but it'll not get replicated to the master indexer here.
So master indexer is solely responsible
for informing or for dictating the peer nodes
about the configuration settings
that it should be adhering to.
Master node, by itself, does not take part
in the replication of data by itself.
So this is one important aspect to remember.
So this is it about today's video.
What we'll be doing in the upcoming video is
we'll be designing the infrastructure which will be needed
for master indexer and for two peer nodes.
And once we have the infrastructure ready,
we can go ahead and perform the configuration aspect
for the indexer cluster.
So this is it about today's video.
I hope this has been informative for you
and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Configuring Master Indexer ---

Instructor: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be configuring the first component
of the indexer cluster, which is the master indexer.
Now, we have already seen the indexer cluster architecture.
So, the first thing that we need to configure
is the master indexer.
Now, once this is configured,
then we can go ahead and configure the peer indexer node one
and the peer indexer node two.
Now, speaking about the master indexer,
there are three important pointers that we need to remember.
First is, a cluster has only and only one master node.
So, this is one important part to remember.
At the time, there can only be one master node.
Second is, master node is responsible for coordinating
the activities between the peer nodes within the cluster.
And third important pointer is,
the master node by itself does not store
or replicate the data.
So, these are the three important pointers
with respect to the master indexer node.
So with this, let's open up the Splunk.
So, let's quickly log in here.
So, within my Splunk enterprise, I'll go to settings,
I'll go to indexer clustering,
and we'll enable the indexer clustering.
Now, this thing we'll be selecting the master node,
and we'll click on next.
Now, there are four important settings here.
One is the replication factor, second is search factor,
third is security key, and fourth is cluster label.
So among this, the replication and search factor
is the two most important configuration setting
within the indexer clustering that we need to set.
So, let's go ahead and understand what replication factor
and search factor means.
Now, the replication factor, basically, determines how
many copy of data your cluster will maintain.
Now, this specific configuration setting is a key factor
in determining the cluster's fault tolerance capability.
So, if you take a simple example over here,
let's say that if we want to ensure that the system,
or your cluster, can handle the failure
of up to two peer nodes,
then we must configure the replication factor of three,
which means cluster will store three identical copies
of data on a separate node.
So currently, if you'll see,
there are three peer nodes over here,
and we have the replication factor of three.
That means one copy of data will be stored
in each of the peer nodes.
That, basically, means that there will be total
of three identical copies of data on separate nodes.
So here, even if the node one and node two goes down,
you still have one identical copy
within the node three and your data will be searchable
and your data will be impact.
And hence, replication factor proves to be
one of the very important configuration settings
when you configure your indexer clustering.
The second important factor here is the search factor.
Now, search factor, basically, determines the number
of immediately searchable copies of data
that the cluster maintains.
Now, there are two important concepts.
One is searchable copy, and second is non-searchable copy.
Now, if you typically,
remember during the video of indexes,
where we were discussing about the bucket life cycle,
in the bucket life cycle,
there was something called a frozen bucket.
And, within the frozen bucket,
all you had was the compressed file.
You do not really have the index there.
So, a non-searchable copy is, basically, a plain raw data
without any index file.
All right, so this is what a non-searchable copy means.
Searchable copy, basically, means it contains the raw data,
and it also contains extensive index files,
so that the searching becomes much more faster and scalable.
So, that's the difference
between searchable copy and non-searchable copy.
So, now that we understood the replication factor
and the search factor,
we can go ahead and configure both of them.
So, let's do one thing, the replication factor,
since we are following this architecture
where we have two nodes, and what we want is that
if one node goes down, we also want a second node.
So, basically, we want two identical copies
of data to be present.
So, we'll put the replication factor of two.
Now, a search factor is, basically, we already discussed.
One important pointer to remember within the search factor
is that non-searchable copy of data will utilize
lesser disk space when compared
with the searchable copy of data.
This is important part to remember.
So, you can configure the search factor.
I'll keep it as two.
The next configuration is the security key.
Now, security keys, basically, useful for authenticating
the communication that happens
between master node, like, communication that happens
between master node peer nodes,

--- Configuration Bundles of Master Indexers ---

-: Hi everyone and welcome back.
Now in today's video we will be discussing
about the configuration bundle.
Now one important part that you need to remember
specifically when you have
a indexer clustered based architecture is
that you should avoid making manual changes
in the peer node.
For example, let's say that you want to go ahead
and create a new index.
You should never create the index at a peer node level.
For example, let's say that you go ahead
and create a index called KP labs in the peer node 0-1.
Then there are certain configurations
like the index that will not be replicated
across multiple peers.
So this index of KP labs will stay at a peer index
and node level here.
And hence it is recommended
that whenever you have some configurations
that will be common to all of the peers
you should go ahead and push it via the master indexer.
So let's say that you want to create a new index,
called KP labs.
You go ahead and define it at a master indexer level.
And master indexer let it push
to all of the peer nodes.
And this is the right way of doing things.
So this common set of configurations
that you will be pushing to all of the peer indexer nodes
is referred to as a configuration bundle.
Now there is a specific location of configuration bundle
for both master and peer indexer.
So in master indexxer, at whatever configuration
that you want to push across all of the peers
you need to store it under the ETC master apps.
So you have Splunk Home.
Now again, important part to remember in documentation
whenever you'll see,
they'll always refer to it as a variable.
So this is kind of a variable of Splunk Home
primarily because depending about the operating system,
this path might change.
So for Linux servers, this would be OPD Splunk directory.
For Windows machine it would be C drive,
program files, et cetera.
So this is the variable.
And under this you have ATC master apps.
So this is where you go ahead
and define your configuration bundle.
And once you push that configuration bundle
to the peer indexer,
you will see those under the ETC slave apps directly.

--- Forwarding Logs to Indexer Cluster ---

-: How you doing and welcome back.
So in today's video, we'll be discussing
about the approaches in which the universal forwarder
can send the logs to the indexer cluster.
Now, do note that there are two ways
in which the universal forwarder can send the logs
to the peer indexer node.
First one is the indexer discovery feature
which is the recommended approach,
and the second one is where the forwarders
directly connect to the peer nodes
by referencing to their configuration file.
Now, in today's video, we'll be using the second approach
to understand more about it,
and then in the next video,
we'll be looking into the indexer discovery feature.
Now, in the second approach that we're discussing
which is not the recommended one,
but still, you will see this in a lot of organizations,
is where the IP addresses of the peer indexes are hard-coded
within the universal forwarder's configuration.
So let's say that you have a universal forwarder
installed in a specific system.
Inside its configuration file,
you will see the IP addresses
of all of the peer indexes that are hard-coded.
So there are two peer indexes that are part of the cluster
and both the IP addresses of these indexes
are hard-coded over here,
and the universal forwarder will pick the IP address
and then it will start to upload the data over here.
Let's say in case if one of the peer indexer goes down,
let's say that you have a 0.5
and this peer indexer goes down.
So then, the universal forwarder
will pick the 0.6 base peer indexer
and it will start to upload data there.
So this is the basics of the hard-coded configuration.
Now, one of the challenges here
is that let's say tomorrow,
there is one more peer indexer node that has come up,
then this universal forwarder does not have any details
about this peer indexer node.
So there can be thousands of universal forwarders.
None of the thousand universal forwarders
will upload the data to the new peer indexer node,
primarily, because it does not really have the IP addresses
within the configuration.
And this is one of the reasons
why it is not a recommended approach.

--- Implementing Indexer Discovery ---

-: Hi everyone, and welcome back.
Now in today's video,
we will be discussing about the indexer discovery.
Now in the indexer discovery based method,
the IP addresses of each indexer peer nodes
are not hard-coded within the configuration.
Instead what happens is that each universal forwarder
will make a query to the master indexer
asking it for the updated list
of the peer indexes that are connected.
For example, let's say that you have a universal forwarder
that is installed in this laptop.
So this universal forwarder,
if it is using the indexer discovery based method,
it will first go ahead and
connect to the master indexer.
And then it will ask for the
list of the peer nodes.
So there are three peer nodes
that are currently available
and master indexer will go ahead
and send a list of the peer nodes
which is Splunk idx1, idx2, and idx3.
And then it uses the load balancing method
to forward the data to the set of peer nodes.
So this is one of the recommended methods
in production environments.
So let's go ahead and look into
how we can achieve this.

--- Overview of Search Head Clustering ---

-: Hi everyone and welcome back.
Now in today's video we will be discussing
about the search head clustering.
Now at this stage we have already set up the indexer cluster
and there are three primary components here.
First one is the master indexer
and then you have the two peer indexer nodes.
Now all of the data is residing in this indexer nodes.
Now if you want to search the data,
what you can do you can go to the master indexer,
and from there you can search the data
that is indexed across the peer nodes.
Now at this stage, you should note
that the indexer nodes are primarily designed
to index the data from multiple sources.
So it is recommended that you make sure
that it remains that way.
Now, first one is the indexing of data.
That is the first primary component.
Second primary component is searching of data.
So the search
and reporting is a resource intensive application
and it can slow down a indexer node
if large queries are ran.
Now since indexer notes are typically designed
to index the data,
there can be thousands of universal forwarder
in your environment that might be sending the data
to the indexer nodes.
And if you log into indexer nodes
and run large search queries or design certain dashboards
it will slow down and affect your entire Splunk environment.
And hence it is recommended
that you should avoid the searching of data
from the indexer cluster component.
So the responsibility of overall search management
is the aim of the search head component.
So the search head component
is responsible to handle the search management functions
directing the search queries to a set of search peers
and also merging the result back to the users.
So all the users who wants to search some data
from your Splunk environment will go ahead
and first log into the search head based Splunk instance.
This search head based Splunk instance
is connected to your entire indexer cluster
and whatever queries that user will run at a searcher level
will be forwarded to the indexer cluster.
The results will be phased back.
It will be merged and it'll be given back to the users.
Now, generally in the environment
that we had basically set up
where we had a set of indexer cluster
and we have a MIDX machine.
So if you look into the indexer clustering console
we have one peer that is up.
And if you look into the search at this Splunk MIDX,
0-1 is acting as a search head.
So any data that you want to search
you search it from the search head
and MIDX is taking the responsibility
in this specific architecture.
Now, generally in smaller organizations
where budget is a constraint,
you will see that MIDX machine will act as a search head.
Users will log in here and perform the search operations.
However, for professional enterprise instances
this should not be the case.
You should go ahead
and have a dedicated search head instances.
Now one more important part to remember
is that let's say that there is one Splunk instance
that is acting as a search head.
And if that Splunk instance goes down,
then the users essentially
will not be able to do anything there.
So it is important that you also
set up a clustered architecture
for the search head component
similar to how you have set up the clustered architecture
for the indexer component.
Now, if you look into the clustered architecture here
of the search head component.
So this is basically the search head cluster
where you have multiple set of instances
that are acting as the cluster members.
And these instances will go ahead
and connect to the search peer.
Search peers are basically the indexer peers
where the actual data resides
and the cluster members which are part
of the search head cluster
will phase their data from the search peers
and give it back to the users over here.
Great. So this part is easy to understand.
Now, one more important part that you need to note
is that there is one more component
that you see on the right called the deployer.
Now, similar to the indexer cluster
where you have a set of peer indexer nodes
and you also had a set of master indexer nodes.
This master indexer node was responsible
for pushing a configuration bundle to the indexer peers.
We have already seen this practically
in the earlier set of videos.
Similar to that, for the search head component
you also have a deployer.
Now the deployer is basically used to push
the configuration bundle to the search head cluster.
So let's say that you want to have a AWS application
in the set of search head cluster nodes.
What you will do, you'll go ahead
and tell the deployer to push this AWS app to search head.
And this deployer will go ahead
and push the entire configuration bundle
to all of the search head node.
So the responsibility of configuration bundle push
is very similar to that of the master indexer over here.
Great. So this is a very high level overview
about the search head clustering.
We'll go ahead and implement the entire cluster architecture
along with deploy in the upcoming videos.
So with this, we'll conclude today's video.

--- Validating Search Head Replication ---

-: Hi everyone and welcome back.
Now that we have set up
the search head clustering based architecture,
one important thing that you need to notice
that between the set of cluster members,
there are certain configuration
and settings that are replicated over here.
For example, if you modify a few things
or if you add few things in one of the cluster members,
it will be replicated across all of the cluster members.
Now, there are certain things that are replicated by default
like alert actions, data models, users, macros, lookups,
even types, and so on.
So whenever you do these things
in any one of the search head members,
it will be replicated among all of them.
Now at this stage we have two search head members
that are available, SS01 and SS02.
So what we'll be doing,
we'll be taking up one of the example from here
and we'll see whether the things are replicated or not.

and we'll create a new user.

Perfect, so we have the SS02 user.

let's refresh this page.

So I hope at a high level overview,

between the search head cluster members is all about.
Now, generally also note that
once you have set up the search head
within your environment,
you will not give any log in credentials
to the indexer machines,
to the deployer machines, et cetera.
The only log in credentials which users would have
would be of that of the search head.
Other than that, there is no other credentials
that needs to be given.
And we already know if you just create a new user
in any of the search head cluster member,
it will be replicated.
So the end user
who wants to do searching on your Splunk environment,

which is the search head
and from search head, they can go ahead
and do the entire searching related capability.
Now at this stage, the search head is not really connected

--- Using Btool for Troublshooting ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video we will be discussing
about the BTool utility.
Now the BTool utility is generally used during the time
of troubleshooting, whenever something is not working
or there are some kind of configuration issues.
So let's understand more about what BTool is.
Now, since we already know
that Splunk Enterprise configuration file system
it supports many overlapping configuration files
which can be present in multiple locations.
Now this can be understood
with one of the images that we had seen during
the configuration precedence video
where you can have same configuration,
like you have server name as king.
In local, you have server name as in the app
and you have server name as default in default directory.
So we know that the local directory will always
have a higher precedence
than the app and the default directory.
So if you want to see that a specific configuration,
say, somehow Splunk is taking up
server name is equal to app, you want to see
from which configuration file this settings
is being picked up from, or which is the configuration
which a Splunk has picked up from.
All of those data will be queried
from the utility called as BTool.
So BTool is basically a command line tool that allows us
to troubleshoot configuration file issues,
to see what values are being pulled up
by the Splunk Enterprise.
So what BTool does is it can allow you
to show the merged settings of all the configuration files.
So let's understand this with the practical example.
So I'm logged into my Splunk system right now.
So let's go to OPT, Splunk, etc system local.
Now if I do a LS, I have inputs dot conf.
So let's do a VI and here you see the host is
equal to Splunk hyphen master.
So now if you quickly come out of this
and let's look into how BTool loads.
So you do a OPT, Splunk bin Splunk, you do a CMD,
you do a BTool, and if you just press enter,
it will basically give you certain options
which we can use with BTool.
So we are more interested to see the configuration
which is there in input.
So this inputs dot conf can be present
in the default directory.

--- Overview of Data Models ---

-: Hey everyone, and welcome back.
In today's video, we'll have a high-level overview
about the data model functionality in Splunk.
Now, before we go ahead and understand data model,
let's look into one of the basic problems
that you'll typically see in organization
which are using Splunk.
Now, it might happen that certain data
that you have indexed in Splunk,
it would contain certain critical business indicators.
However, many times what happens is that data
which contains those critical business indicators
is multilayer, and it is complicated.
So it has multiple layers and it is very complicated
to read and to understand directly.
Now, the users who wants to see these business indicators
from this complicated dataset are typically less technical,
they are not master of the SPL command.
So now what happens is that data structure is complicated.
The users who wants to get some critical business indicators
from that data are less technical in Splunk.
So they'll not be able to write those complex queries.
And you don't want to be point of contact for everything
they might want to do from that data.
So what you basically do is you make use of data models.
So one of the simple example
is that if you look into this search query,
it is quite long.
And this in fact is a short search query.
I have seen search queries which are like double the size
of the search query which is written over here.
And you'll not expect some normal Splunk user
to write such a complex query, right?
And basically, these queries might fetch
the required details from some complicated data structure,
but this queries are something which a normal person
or normal user will not write.
So what you basically do is you make use of data model
to provide some meaningful information
from the underlying data.
So let's say this is a data model over here
called as sourcetype, and there are three subsets of it.
One is success, you have failure, and you have warning.
So it becomes much more simpler for anyone
who wants to make some information from this dataset.
He can just click on the success model
and he'll know that it will only contain information
or relevant information which are required
for the success related datasets.
Same for failure, and same for warning.
Now, one simple example can be understood here
where you have a customer and you have divided
into three subsets.
One is order history, second is web history,
third is problem tickets.
So when you click on problem ticket datasets,
you'll find all the data which contains
the problematic tickets.
When you look into the order history,
you'll get all the data which contains the order history.
So this is one of the ways
in which the data model can be built.
So let's understand on how this might look
in a practical manner in form of screenshots.
So this is a Vulnerability data model.
Now, if you see this data model,
it is divided into three subsets.
One is high or critical vulnerability.
Second is medium vulnerability.
And third is low or informational vulnerability.
Now as a end user, let's say I am little technical
in terms of Splunk, I do not know much about Splunk.
But from this information, I can say,
"Okay, if I want to see critical level vulnerabilities,
I can click over here."
And basically what it'll do
is it'll take me to a new Pivoting page.
Basically, if you do a pivot,
we'll be looking this into the upcoming video,
it will take me to a new page,
and it'll show the count of higher/critical vulnerabilities.
Here it says that total amount
of higher critical vulnerabilities is 4904.
And basically on the left hand side,
if you just click on here,
it'll create the dashboards for you,
you don't really have to write the SPL commands.
You just click here,
and the dashboards will be built automatically for you.
So in the next slide,
you see the dashboards are automatically built
that these are the amount of vulnerabilities.
Like from October 20 to 29,
there were good amount of vulnerabilities
with a peak of close to 1600 vulnerabilities
of class higher/critical which were present.
So for a end user who is less technical,
without writing any SPL commands,
without writing any query
to find higher critical vulnerabilities,
just by point and click,
he'll be able to retrieve all the relevant data
which is required for his use case to happen.
And this is one of the functions of why data model
is generally used in a lot of organizations.
So that's about it for this video.
In the next video, we'll look into how we can build
our own data model.

--- Splunk Support Programs ---

-: Hey everyone and welcome back.
In today's video we'll be discussing
about these Splunk official support programs.
Now, typically Splunk provides
official support for customers.
So in case your Splunk instance is not working as expected
you can officially contact them
through a support portal and their team will help you.
Now, their support programs are primarily divided
into following categories.
One is community, second is base, third is standard
and fourth is premium.
So let's look into all of them
and understand the difference.
So this is the Splunk support programs page
and if you go a bit down
you'll see that there are four categories
community, base, standard, and premium.
Now for the base and for the community
you do not really have the support.
It's the community support that you can get from the base.
You can provide your tickets
but you will not really have any response time targets.
So the only thing
if you are an enterprise that you should be looking for
is either the standard one or the premium one.
So typically when you go ahead and buy Splunk Enterprise
the standard support plan will be included
in the pricing that you will be paying for.
However, premium support is available.
So either you can subscribe it, it will be expensive
but this is something that you can subscribe for.
Premium support does comes with the direct access
to the advance support team in case required.
Now, from what I have observed from standard support
their support is good, I would not say the best,
but it is good.
So I have never tried the premium support as far
as the organizations that I have worked is concerned.
But many times what happens
in standard support is that they go with the work around.
They'll not give you the exact pinpoint RC
on why the issue had caused.
They'll just say, okay, this is the way you can fix this
but they'll never tell you on why it occurred
at the first place.
So that is one thing as a personal experience
that I have seen it to be missing in standard support.
Maybe for Splunk premium support.
The advanced supporting might be able to tell you that
but again, this is something which is expensive.
So one important part to remember over here
is that their support is divided into priorities.
Now these priorities depend upon how urgent it is
or what is the impact of the issues
making to your Splunk installations.

that production installation of Splunk software

So for the base, you have the response time

the response time becomes much more lower.

we had to raise p1

it's better to use the stable version.

you will find some unexpected issues there.
So whenever you raise a p1 ticket,

As far as a p1 ticket is concerned,
for p2, p2 is something where
some of the important features are inaccessible
